========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. “Intrusive” is a word I hear patients use, and I know what it’s supposed to mean, but when it’s happening to me it doesn’t feel that clean.

It feels unwanted, yes, in the sense that I don’t want to be thinking about it. But it also feels like… like maybe I’m obligated to think about it, if that makes sense. Like if I stop turning it over, I’m being careless. I don’t know if that counts as suppressing or neutralizing.

Sometimes I try to distract myself, or I’ll go back through the situation in my head, but then I get pulled into the details—like one note I wrote last week, or the way someone phrased something in handover—and then I’m not really answering your question anymore. So… I guess I don’t know. I notice the thoughts, and I don’t like them, but I’m not sure what category they fit into.

How can I help you today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I keep feeling like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. Especially at work. I’m a doctor, so people just sort of hand you this enormous responsibility, and lately I keep replaying small things—like tiny clinical imperfections, things other people might say are normal—and I turn them over and over until they feel like proof that I’m dangerous, like I shouldn’t be allowed to do this.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds dramatic. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t know if I’d say I’m “driven” exactly. I do go back over things in my head, but it’s hard to tell whether I’m trying to reduce anxiety or whether I’m just… thinking, like any careful doctor would.

Sometimes the line feels blurry. Like, in residency we were always told to double-check everything, and that becomes part of you. I had this senior once who could remember every lab value from rounds, and I used to think that was what competence looked like. So now when I replay something, I can tell myself it’s just diligence, but then it gets sort of sticky and I’m still there mentally long after it’s useful.

But whether I’m doing it to prevent something bad from happening… I’m not sure. I guess I haven’t thought of it that way.

I’m not sure I can say it’s about making everything perfect. I mean, I do go back over details sometimes, but… medicine trains you to do that, doesn’t it? It’s hard to separate what’s reasonable from what’s me getting stuck.

I’m not sure. I can think of times I look back at things, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a pattern like… “if I don’t do this, something bad will happen.” It’s not that clear in my head.

And with labs and notes, I mean, everyone checks things twice sometimes. There was a whole culture around that where I trained—people would praise you for being meticulous, and then later it becomes hard to tell when meticulous is just meticulous versus when it’s… something else.

So I guess I don’t really know. I don’t think I’m doing some specific repeated thing to calm anxiety, at least not that I can point to. I just get caught thinking about it sometimes.

I don’t know that I can separate those out cleanly. I’m a doctor, so people just sort of hand you this enormous responsibility, and lately I keep replaying small things—like tiny clinical imperfections, things other people might say are normal—and I turn them over and over until they feel like proof that I’m dangerous, like I shouldn’t be allowed to do this.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds dramatic. It’s just… even when nothing major happened, I can’t shake the feeling that it means something terrible about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about not being good enough or being dangerous come up repeatedly, even when there’s no clear reason for them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, they do. It turns into this… certainty almost, that I’m dangerous somehow, or that I don’t deserve the responsibility people give me.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel intrusive and unwanted, like they’re not quite your own, and do you try to suppress or neutralize them in some way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. “Intrusive” is a word I hear patients use, and I know what it’s supposed to mean, but when it’s happening to me it doesn’t feel that clean.\n\nIt feels unwanted, yes, in the sense that I don’t want to be thinking about it. But it also feels like… like maybe I’m obligated to think about it, if that makes sense. Like if I stop turning it over, I’m being careless. I don’t know if that counts as suppressing or neutralizing.\n\nSometimes I try to distract myself, or I’ll go back through the situation in my head, but then I get pulled into the details—like one note I wrote last week, or the way someone phrased something in handover—and then I’m not really answering your question anymore. So… I guess I don’t know. I notice the thoughts, and I don’t like them, but I’m not sure what category they fit into.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you feel driven to do something—like checking, reviewing, or trying to fix the situation in your mind—to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say I’m “driven” exactly. I do go back over things in my head, but it’s hard to tell whether I’m trying to reduce anxiety or whether I’m just… thinking, like any careful doctor would.\n\nSometimes the line feels blurry. Like, in residency we were always told to double-check everything, and that becomes part of you. I had this senior once who could remember every lab value from rounds, and I used to think that was what competence looked like. I guess I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you feel a strong need to do something—like re-reading notes, double-checking decisions, or going over the details again and again—to make sure everything is perfect or to ease your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it’s about making everything perfect. I mean, I do go back over details sometimes, but… medicine trains you to do that, doesn’t it? It’s hard to separate what’s reasonable from what’s me getting stuck.\n\nLike, I’ll think about a note or a decision and then suddenly I’m remembering something an attending said years ago about how missed details are where mistakes happen, and then I’m not really checking anything concrete anymore, I’m just… circling it. I can think of times I look back at things, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a pattern like… “if I don’t do this, something bad will happen.” It’s not that clear in my head.\n\nAnd with labs and notes, I mean, everyone checks things twice sometimes. There was a whole culture around that where I trained—people would praise you for being meticulous, and then later it becomes hard to tell when meticulous is just meticulous versus when it’s… something else.\n\nSo I guess I don’t really know. I had an attending who used to say if you’re not uncomfortable, you’re not paying attention, which sounds wise until you’re lying awake thinking about some tiny detail from a chart.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I don’t know. “Just right” is hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t say I’m chasing that exact feeling, like a switch flips and suddenly it’s okay. It’s just… even when nothing major happened, I can’t shake the feeling that it means something terrible about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about not being good enough or being dangerous come up repeatedly, even when there’s no clear reason for them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, they do. So… I guess I don’t know. I notice the thoughts, and I don’t like them, but I’m not sure what category they fit into.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you feel driven to do something—like checking, reviewing, or trying to fix the situation in your mind—to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say I’m “driven” exactly. I do go back over things in my head, but it’s hard to tell whether I’m trying to reduce anxiety or whether I’m just… thinking, like any careful doctor would.\n\nSometimes the line feels blurry. I had this senior once who could remember every lab value from rounds, and I used to think that was what competence looked like. I’m not sure I’d call it anxious in that exact way. Distressed, maybe, but it’s hard to tell if that’s because I think something will go wrong or because I’ve just been thinking about it too long.

Sometimes I’ll be going over something and then suddenly I’m remembering all these cases people talked about in training—like the cautionary tales, the “one missed detail” stories—and those kind of stick in your head. But I don’t know that I have a clear thought of, “If I don’t get this right, something bad will happen.” It’s more murky than that.

I guess I do feel unsettled while I’m doing it, but I can’t really say what I’m afraid of, if that makes sense.

Sometimes it feels like wanting to be certain, but then I start thinking about what “certain” even means in clinical work, because there’s always ambiguity. I had an attending who used to say if you’re not uncomfortable, you’re not paying attention, which sounds wise until you’re lying awake thinking about some tiny detail from a chart.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “something terrible will happen if I don’t check this.” It’s more that I get caught in the reviewing, and the more I review, the less settled it feels. I wouldn’t say I’m chasing that exact feeling, like a switch flips and suddenly it’s okay. It’s more… I start turning something over, and then I remember how in training there was always this emphasis on not missing the one small thing, you know? I do go back over things in my head, but it’s hard to tell whether I’m trying to reduce anxiety or whether I’m just… thinking, like any careful doctor would.\n\nSometimes the line feels blurry. Like, in residency we were always told to double-check everything, and that becomes part of you. I had this senior once who could remember every lab value from rounds, and I used to think that was what competence looked like. So now when I replay something, I can tell myself it’s just diligence, but then it gets sort of sticky and I’m still there mentally long after it’s useful.\n\nBut whether I’m doing it to prevent something bad from happening… I’m not sure. I guess I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you feel a strong need to do something—like re-reading notes, double-checking decisions, or going over the details again and again—to make sure everything is perfect or to ease your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it’s about making everything perfect. I mean, I do go back over details sometimes, but… medicine trains you to do that, doesn’t it? It’s hard to separate what’s reasonable from what’s me getting stuck.\n\nLike, I’ll think about a note or a decision and then suddenly I’m remembering something an attending said years ago about how missed details are where mistakes happen, and then I’m not really checking anything concrete anymore, I’m just… circling it. But whether that eases my mind? Not really. Or maybe briefly. I don’t know.\n\nI haven’t noticed a clear ritual, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I get pulled into reviewing things, and then I lose track of whether it’s useful.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly doing certain things—like re-reading notes, checking lab results multiple times, or going through decisions again and again in your head—not necessarily because you need to, but because it somehow feels necessary to avoid something bad happening or to calm the anxiety these thoughts bring up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can think of times I look back at things, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a pattern like… “if I don’t do this, something bad will happen.” It’s not that clear in my head.\n\nAnd with labs and notes, I mean, everyone checks things twice sometimes. There was a whole culture around that where I trained—people would praise you for being meticulous, and then later it becomes hard to tell when meticulous is just meticulous versus when it’s… something else.\n\nSo I guess I don’t really know. I don’t think I’m doing some specific repeated thing to calm anxiety, at least not that I can point to. I just get caught thinking about it sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself going over decisions or clinical details again and again, does it feel driven by a need to be certain that everything is right, or by a fear that something terrible might happen if you miss something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can separate those out cleanly. Sometimes it feels like wanting to be certain, but then I start thinking about what “certain” even means in clinical work, because there’s always ambiguity. I had an attending who used to say if you’re not uncomfortable, you’re not paying attention, which sounds wise until you’re lying awake thinking about some tiny detail from a chart.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “something terrible will happen if I don’t check this.” It’s more that I get caught in the reviewing, and the more I review, the less settled it feels. I can’t really tell if it’s fear or just habit or… trying to be careful.\n\nDoctor: When you go over clinical details or decisions repeatedly in your mind, does it feel like you’re trying to reach a sense of certainty or 'just right' feeling, even if you can’t clearly explain why it feels important?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Just right” is hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t say I’m chasing that exact feeling, like a switch flips and suddenly it’s okay. It’s more… I start turning something over, and then I remember how in training there was always this emphasis on not missing the one small thing, you know? Like the whole case could hinge on some lab value buried in the chart.\n\nBut I’m not sure that means I’m trying to get certainty. Sometimes I think I’m just trying to feel less uneasy, and sometimes I’m not even sure what I’m trying to do anymore. It doesn’t really land in a clear way for me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re going over these clinical details or decisions in your mind, do you feel anxious or distressed while you’re doing it, like something might go wrong if you don’t get it 'right'?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious in that exact way. Distressed, maybe, but it’s hard to tell if that’s because I think something will go wrong or because I’ve just been thinking about it too long.\n\nSometimes I’ll be going over something and then suddenly I’m remembering all these cases people talked about in training—like the cautionary tales, the “one missed detail” stories—and those kind of stick in your head. But I don’t know that I have a clear thought of, “If I don’t get this right, something bad will happen.” It’s more murky than that.\n\nI guess I do feel unsettled while I’m doing it, but I can’t really say what I’m afraid of, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself deliberately doing something—like re-reading notes, checking lab results, or going through decisions in your head in a specific way or for a certain amount of time—to try to reduce your distress or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do it in a structured way, if that’s what you mean. Like, I’m not counting how many times I read something or following a rule exactly.\n\nI might look back at something, or think through it again, but it’s hard to say whether I’m doing it to prevent something bad or just because my mind gets stuck on it. In the hospital, everyone checks things, you know—labs, notes, sign-outs—and sometimes it’s just part of the culture of not wanting to be sloppy. Though I guess that doesn’t really answer your question.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a specific ritual or set amount of time. It’s more vague than that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it has, though I keep wanting to qualify that because I’m still going in, I’m still showing up to clinic. It’s not like I’ve stopped working.

But the edges are fraying a bit. I’ve had to ask other people to help me with some of the non-urgent admin stuff, the things I normally would just handle. And then outside of work, I’ve been avoiding plans more. Someone will text about dinner or whatever, and I’ll just… I don’t trust that I’ll have the judgment or the energy to be decent company, I guess.

So yes, it’s started affecting how reliably I function at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people outside of it. Not in some dramatic way, but enough that I notice it.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F42.8, F43.10, F43.11\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. How can I help you today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I keep feeling like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. Especially at work. I’m a doctor, so people just sort of hand you this enormous responsibility, and lately I keep replaying small things—like tiny clinical imperfections, things other people might say are normal—and I turn them over and over until they feel like proof that I’m dangerous, like I shouldn’t be allowed to do this.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds dramatic. It’s just… even when nothing major happened, I can’t shake the feeling that it means something terrible about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about not being good enough or being dangerous come up repeatedly, even when there’s no clear reason for them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, they do. It’s not always tied to something obvious. I’ll be driving home or trying to make dinner and suddenly I’m back on some small decision from work, some minor imperfection, and it starts feeling like evidence that I shouldn’t be trusted.\n\nAnd I know people say, you know, doctors are supposed to review things and be careful—one of my attendings used to say good clinicians worry, the bad ones don’t—but this feels different. It turns into this… certainty almost, that I’m dangerous somehow, or that I don’t deserve the responsibility people give me.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel intrusive and unwanted, like they’re not quite your own, and do you try to suppress or neutralize them in some way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. “Intrusive” is a word I hear patients use, and I know what it’s supposed to mean, but when it’s happening to me it doesn’t feel that clean.\n\nIt feels unwanted, yes, in the sense that I don’t want to be thinking about it. But it also feels like… like maybe I’m obligated to think about it, if that makes sense. Like if I stop turning it over, I’m being careless. I don’t know if that counts as suppressing or neutralizing.\n\nSometimes I try to distract myself, or I’ll go back through the situation in my head, but then I get pulled into the details—like one note I wrote last week, or the way someone phrased something in handover—and then I’m not really answering your question anymore. So… I guess I don’t know. I notice the thoughts, and I don’t like them, but I’m not sure what category they fit into.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you feel driven to do something—like checking, reviewing, or trying to fix the situation in your mind—to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say I’m “driven” exactly. I do go back over things in my head, but it’s hard to tell whether I’m trying to reduce anxiety or whether I’m just… thinking, like any careful doctor would.\n\nSometimes the line feels blurry. Like, in residency we were always told to double-check everything, and that becomes part of you. I had this senior once who could remember every lab value from rounds, and I used to think that was what competence looked like. So now when I replay something, I can tell myself it’s just diligence, but then it gets sort of sticky and I’m still there mentally long after it’s useful.\n\nBut whether I’m doing it to prevent something bad from happening… I’m not sure. I guess I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you feel a strong need to do something—like re-reading notes, double-checking decisions, or going over the details again and again—to make sure everything is perfect or to ease your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it’s about making everything perfect. I mean, I do go back over details sometimes, but… medicine trains you to do that, doesn’t it? It’s hard to separate what’s reasonable from what’s me getting stuck.\n\nLike, I’ll think about a note or a decision and then suddenly I’m remembering something an attending said years ago about how missed details are where mistakes happen, and then I’m not really checking anything concrete anymore, I’m just… circling it. But whether that eases my mind? Not really. Or maybe briefly. I don’t know.\n\nI haven’t noticed a clear ritual, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I get pulled into reviewing things, and then I lose track of whether it’s useful.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly doing certain things—like re-reading notes, checking lab results multiple times, or going through decisions again and again in your head—not necessarily because you need to, but because it somehow feels necessary to avoid something bad happening or to calm the anxiety these thoughts bring up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can think of times I look back at things, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a pattern like… “if I don’t do this, something bad will happen.” It’s not that clear in my head.\n\nAnd with labs and notes, I mean, everyone checks things twice sometimes. There was a whole culture around that where I trained—people would praise you for being meticulous, and then later it becomes hard to tell when meticulous is just meticulous versus when it’s… something else.\n\nSo I guess I don’t really know. I don’t think I’m doing some specific repeated thing to calm anxiety, at least not that I can point to. I just get caught thinking about it sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself going over decisions or clinical details again and again, does it feel driven by a need to be certain that everything is right, or by a fear that something terrible might happen if you miss something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can separate those out cleanly. Sometimes it feels like wanting to be certain, but then I start thinking about what “certain” even means in clinical work, because there’s always ambiguity. I had an attending who used to say if you’re not uncomfortable, you’re not paying attention, which sounds wise until you’re lying awake thinking about some tiny detail from a chart.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “something terrible will happen if I don’t check this.” It’s more that I get caught in the reviewing, and the more I review, the less settled it feels. I can’t really tell if it’s fear or just habit or… trying to be careful.\n\nDoctor: When you go over clinical details or decisions repeatedly in your mind, does it feel like you’re trying to reach a sense of certainty or 'just right' feeling, even if you can’t clearly explain why it feels important?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Just right” is hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t say I’m chasing that exact feeling, like a switch flips and suddenly it’s okay. It’s more… I start turning something over, and then I remember how in training there was always this emphasis on not missing the one small thing, you know? Like the whole case could hinge on some lab value buried in the chart.\n\nBut I’m not sure that means I’m trying to get certainty. Sometimes I think I’m just trying to feel less uneasy, and sometimes I’m not even sure what I’m trying to do anymore. It doesn’t really land in a clear way for me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re going over these clinical details or decisions in your mind, do you feel anxious or distressed while you’re doing it, like something might go wrong if you don’t get it 'right'?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious in that exact way. Distressed, maybe, but it’s hard to tell if that’s because I think something will go wrong or because I’ve just been thinking about it too long.\n\nSometimes I’ll be going over something and then suddenly I’m remembering all these cases people talked about in training—like the cautionary tales, the “one missed detail” stories—and those kind of stick in your head. But I don’t know that I have a clear thought of, “If I don’t get this right, something bad will happen.” It’s more murky than that.\n\nI guess I do feel unsettled while I’m doing it, but I can’t really say what I’m afraid of, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself deliberately doing something—like re-reading notes, checking lab results, or going through decisions in your head in a specific way or for a certain amount of time—to try to reduce your distress or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do it in a structured way, if that’s what you mean. Like, I’m not counting how many times I read something or following a rule exactly.\n\nI might look back at something, or think through it again, but it’s hard to say whether I’m doing it to prevent something bad or just because my mind gets stuck on it. In the hospital, everyone checks things, you know—labs, notes, sign-outs—and sometimes it’s just part of the culture of not wanting to be sloppy. Though I guess that doesn’t really answer your question.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a specific ritual or set amount of time. It’s more vague than that.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts about clinical decisions or details ever interfered with your work, relationships, or daily activities, or caused you significant distress even when you try to let them go?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, though I keep wanting to qualify that because I’m still going in, I’m still showing up to clinic. And then outside of work, I’ve been avoiding plans more. It’s more vague than that.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts about clinical decisions or details ever interfered with your work, relationships, or daily activities, or caused you significant distress even when you try to let them go?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, though I keep wanting to qualify that because I’m still going in, I’m still showing up to clinic. It’s not like I’ve stopped working.\n\nBut the edges are fraying a bit. I’ve had to ask other people to help me with some of the non-urgent admin stuff, the things I normally would just handle. And then outside of work, I’ve been avoiding plans more. Someone will text about dinner or whatever, and I’ll just… I don’t trust that I’ll have the judgment or the energy to be decent company, I guess.\n\nSo yes, it’s started affecting how reliably I function at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people outside of it. Not in some dramatic way, but enough that I notice it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like these thoughts about your work or decisions keep coming back in a way that feels out of your control, even when you try not to think about them?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, they do come back. I mean, not like a pop-up window or something, but… I’ll be doing something else and suddenly I’m back on some tiny clinical detail from earlier, some wording in a note or a decision that probably wasn’t even the main issue.

And then it sort of turns into this bigger thing, like, “See, that’s proof you shouldn’t be trusted.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud. I know physicians are always talking about humility and double-checking, and in training they drill into you that confidence can be dangerous—one of my attendings used to say that constantly, actually, usually right before telling some horror story from residency.

But for me it’s not just learning from mistakes. It feels like I keep replaying small imperfections as if they mean I’m a dangerous doctor. Like I don’t deserve the trust patients or colleagues put in me. I try to move on from it, but it loops back.

It’s just… even when nothing major happened, I can’t shake the feeling that it means something terrible about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about not being good enough or being dangerous come up repeatedly, even when there’s no clear reason for them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, they do. It’s not always tied to something obvious. It turns into this… certainty almost, that I’m dangerous somehow, or that I don’t deserve the responsibility people give me.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel intrusive and unwanted, like they’re not quite your own, and do you try to suppress or neutralize them in some way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. “Intrusive” is a word I hear patients use, and I know what it’s supposed to mean, but when it’s happening to me it doesn’t feel that clean.\n\nIt feels unwanted, yes, in the sense that I don’t want to be thinking about it. So… I guess I don’t know. I notice the thoughts, and I don’t like them, but I’m not sure what category they fit into.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you feel driven to do something—like checking, reviewing, or trying to fix the situation in your mind—to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say I’m “driven” exactly. I do go back over things in my head, but it’s hard to tell whether I’m trying to reduce anxiety or whether I’m just… thinking, like any careful doctor would.\n\nSometimes the line feels blurry. Like, in residency we were always told to double-check everything, and that becomes part of you. I had this senior once who could remember every lab value from rounds, and I used to think that was what competence looked like. So now when I replay something, I can tell myself it’s just diligence, but then it gets sort of sticky and I’m still there mentally long after it’s useful.\n\nBut whether I’m doing it to prevent something bad from happening… I’m not sure. I guess I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you feel a strong need to do something—like re-reading notes, double-checking decisions, or going over the details again and again—to make sure everything is perfect or to ease your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it’s about making everything perfect. I mean, I do go back over details sometimes, but… medicine trains you to do that, doesn’t it? It’s hard to separate what’s reasonable from what’s me getting stuck.\n\nLike, I’ll think about a note or a decision and then suddenly I’m remembering something an attending said years ago about how missed details are where mistakes happen, and then I’m not really checking anything concrete anymore, I’m just… circling it. But whether that eases my mind? Not really. Or maybe briefly. I don’t know.\n\nI haven’t noticed a clear ritual, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I get pulled into reviewing things, and then I lose track of whether it’s useful.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly doing certain things—like re-reading notes, checking lab results multiple times, or going through decisions again and again in your head—not necessarily because you need to, but because it somehow feels necessary to avoid something bad happening or to calm the anxiety these thoughts bring up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can think of times I look back at things, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a pattern like… “if I don’t do this, something bad will happen.” It’s not that clear in my head.\n\nAnd with labs and notes, I mean, everyone checks things twice sometimes. There was a whole culture around that where I trained—people would praise you for being meticulous, and then later it becomes hard to tell when meticulous is just meticulous versus when it’s… something else.\n\nSo I guess I don’t really know. I don’t think I’m doing some specific repeated thing to calm anxiety, at least not that I can point to. I just get caught thinking about it sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself going over decisions or clinical details again and again, does it feel driven by a need to be certain that everything is right, or by a fear that something terrible might happen if you miss something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can separate those out cleanly. Sometimes it feels like wanting to be certain, but then I start thinking about what “certain” even means in clinical work, because there’s always ambiguity. I had an attending who used to say if you’re not uncomfortable, you’re not paying attention, which sounds wise until you’re lying awake thinking about some tiny detail from a chart.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “something terrible will happen if I don’t check this.” It’s more that I get caught in the reviewing, and the more I review, the less settled it feels. I can’t really tell if it’s fear or just habit or… trying to be careful.\n\nDoctor: When you go over clinical details or decisions repeatedly in your mind, does it feel like you’re trying to reach a sense of certainty or 'just right' feeling, even if you can’t clearly explain why it feels important?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Just right” is hard for me to pin down. I don’t think so… not in that specific way. I mean, I do go back over things in my head, but it doesn’t feel like I have a set routine, like “repeat this phrase” or “review it three times” and then I’ll be safe.

Yeah… I think so. Not in the sense that I’m refusing to do my job — I’m still going to clinic, still seeing patients. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.

It’s just… even when nothing major happened, I can’t shake the feeling that it means something terrible about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about not being good enough or being dangerous come up repeatedly, even when there’s no clear reason for them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, they do. I’ll be driving home or trying to make dinner and suddenly I’m back on some small decision from work, some minor imperfection, and it starts feeling like evidence that I shouldn’t be trusted.\n\nAnd I know people say, you know, doctors are supposed to review things and be careful—one of my attendings used to say good clinicians worry, the bad ones don’t—but this feels different. It turns into this… certainty almost, that I’m dangerous somehow, or that I don’t deserve the responsibility people give me.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel intrusive and unwanted, like they’re not quite your own, and do you try to suppress or neutralize them in some way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. “Intrusive” is a word I hear patients use, and I know what it’s supposed to mean, but when it’s happening to me it doesn’t feel that clean.\n\nIt feels unwanted, yes, in the sense that I don’t want to be thinking about it. But it also feels like… like maybe I’m obligated to think about it, if that makes sense. Like if I stop turning it over, I’m being careless. I don’t know if that counts as suppressing or neutralizing.\n\nSometimes I try to distract myself, or I’ll go back through the situation in my head, but then I get pulled into the details—like one note I wrote last week, or the way someone phrased something in handover—and then I’m not really answering your question anymore. So… I guess I don’t know. I notice the thoughts, and I don’t like them, but I’m not sure what category they fit into.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you feel driven to do something—like checking, reviewing, or trying to fix the situation in your mind—to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say I’m “driven” exactly. I do go back over things in my head, but it’s hard to tell whether I’m trying to reduce anxiety or whether I’m just… thinking, like any careful doctor would.\n\nSometimes the line feels blurry. Like, in residency we were always told to double-check everything, and that becomes part of you. I had this senior once who could remember every lab value from rounds, and I used to think that was what competence looked like. So now when I replay something, I can tell myself it’s just diligence, but then it gets sort of sticky and I’m still there mentally long after it’s useful.\n\nBut whether I’m doing it to prevent something bad from happening… I’m not sure. I guess I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you feel a strong need to do something—like re-reading notes, double-checking decisions, or going over the details again and again—to make sure everything is perfect or to ease your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it’s about making everything perfect. I mean, I do go back over details sometimes, but… medicine trains you to do that, doesn’t it? It’s hard to separate what’s reasonable from what’s me getting stuck.\n\nLike, I’ll think about a note or a decision and then suddenly I’m remembering something an attending said years ago about how missed details are where mistakes happen, and then I’m not really checking anything concrete anymore, I’m just… circling it. But whether that eases my mind? Not really. Or maybe briefly. I don’t know.\n\nI haven’t noticed a clear ritual, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I get pulled into reviewing things, and then I lose track of whether it’s useful.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly doing certain things—like re-reading notes, checking lab results multiple times, or going through decisions again and again in your head—not necessarily because you need to, but because it somehow feels necessary to avoid something bad happening or to calm the anxiety these thoughts bring up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can think of times I look back at things, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a pattern like… “if I don’t do this, something bad will happen.” It’s not that clear in my head.\n\nAnd with labs and notes, I mean, everyone checks things twice sometimes. There was a whole culture around that where I trained—people would praise you for being meticulous, and then later it becomes hard to tell when meticulous is just meticulous versus when it’s… something else.\n\nSo I guess I don’t really know. I don’t think I’m doing some specific repeated thing to calm anxiety, at least not that I can point to. I just get caught thinking about it sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself going over decisions or clinical details again and again, does it feel driven by a need to be certain that everything is right, or by a fear that something terrible might happen if you miss something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can separate those out cleanly. Sometimes it feels like wanting to be certain, but then I start thinking about what “certain” even means in clinical work, because there’s always ambiguity. I had an attending who used to say if you’re not uncomfortable, you’re not paying attention, which sounds wise until you’re lying awake thinking about some tiny detail from a chart.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “something terrible will happen if I don’t check this.” It’s more that I get caught in the reviewing, and the more I review, the less settled it feels. I can’t really tell if it’s fear or just habit or… trying to be careful.\n\nDoctor: When you go over clinical details or decisions repeatedly in your mind, does it feel like you’re trying to reach a sense of certainty or 'just right' feeling, even if you can’t clearly explain why it feels important?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Just right” is hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t say I’m chasing that exact feeling, like a switch flips and suddenly it’s okay. It’s more… I start turning something over, and then I remember how in training there was always this emphasis on not missing the one small thing, you know? Like the whole case could hinge on some lab value buried in the chart.\n\nBut I’m not sure that means I’m trying to get certainty. Sometimes I think I’m just trying to feel less uneasy, and sometimes I’m not even sure what I’m trying to do anymore. It doesn’t really land in a clear way for me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re going over these clinical details or decisions in your mind, do you feel anxious or distressed while you’re doing it, like something might go wrong if you don’t get it 'right'?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious in that exact way. Distressed, maybe, but it’s hard to tell if that’s because I think something will go wrong or because I’ve just been thinking about it too long.\n\nSometimes I’ll be going over something and then suddenly I’m remembering all these cases people talked about in training—like the cautionary tales, the “one missed detail” stories—and those kind of stick in your head. But I don’t know that I have a clear thought of, “If I don’t get this right, something bad will happen.” It’s more murky than that.\n\nI guess I do feel unsettled while I’m doing it, but I can’t really say what I’m afraid of, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself deliberately doing something—like re-reading notes, checking lab results, or going through decisions in your head in a specific way or for a certain amount of time—to try to reduce your distress or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do it in a structured way, if that’s what you mean. Like, I’m not counting how many times I read something or following a rule exactly.\n\nI might look back at something, or think through it again, but it’s hard to say whether I’m doing it to prevent something bad or just because my mind gets stuck on it. In the hospital, everyone checks things, you know—labs, notes, sign-outs—and sometimes it’s just part of the culture of not wanting to be sloppy. Though I guess that doesn’t really answer your question.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a specific ritual or set amount of time. It’s more vague than that.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts about clinical decisions or details ever interfered with your work, relationships, or daily activities, or caused you significant distress even when you try to let them go?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, though I keep wanting to qualify that because I’m still going in, I’m still showing up to clinic. It’s not like I’ve stopped working.\n\nBut the edges are fraying a bit. I’ve had to ask other people to help me with some of the non-urgent admin stuff, the things I normally would just handle. And then outside of work, I’ve been avoiding plans more. Someone will text about dinner or whatever, and I’ll just… I don’t trust that I’ll have the judgment or the energy to be decent company, I guess.\n\nSo yes, it’s started affecting how reliably I function at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people outside of it. Not in some dramatic way, but enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like these thoughts about your work or decisions keep coming back in a way that feels out of your control, even when you try not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do come back. I mean, not like a pop-up window or something, but… I’ll be doing something else and suddenly I’m back on some tiny clinical detail from earlier, some wording in a note or a decision that probably wasn’t even the main issue.\n\nAnd then it sort of turns into this bigger thing, like, “See, that’s proof you shouldn’t be trusted.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud. I know physicians are always talking about humility and double-checking, and in training they drill into you that confidence can be dangerous—one of my attendings used to say that constantly, actually, usually right before telling some horror story from residency.\n\nBut for me it’s not just learning from mistakes. It feels like I keep replaying small imperfections as if they mean I’m a dangerous doctor. Like I don’t deserve the trust patients or colleagues put in me. I try to move on from it, but it loops back.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to mentally go over something in a specific way, like repeating certain thoughts or following a mental routine, to try to feel better or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that specific way. I mean, I do go back over things in my head, but it doesn’t feel like I have a set routine, like “repeat this phrase” or “review it three times” and then I’ll be safe.\n\nIt’s more messy than that. Like my brain just wanders back to it, and then I’m stuck there for a while. But I’m not deliberately doing a mental ritual, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI guess in medicine there’s so much internal rehearsal anyway—presenting patients, anticipating questions, thinking through differentials—and sometimes that blurs together. But no, I can’t point to a specific mental routine I feel compelled to follow.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts ever led you to avoid certain situations, responsibilities, or interactions because you were worried about not being able to manage them properly or being judged?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in the sense that I’m refusing to do my job — I’m still going to clinic, still seeing patients. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut I have been sort of sidestepping things. Like some of the non-urgent admin tasks, paperwork-type things I’d normally just knock out, I’ve asked other people for help with because I don’t feel like I can trust myself to handle everything cleanly right now. Which is embarrassing, because it’s not even the hard part of the job, you know?\n\nAnd socially, yes. I’ve been avoiding plans. Someone will suggest dinner or meeting up, and I think, I’m going to be strange, or tired, or I won’t have the judgment to respond like a normal person. Then I just don’t go, or I let the text sit there too long.\n\nSo… yes, I guess I am avoiding some responsibilities and interactions. I’m still functioning, but less reliably than I’d like, and I feel pretty disconnected outside of work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "is_final": false

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S019\n  name: Delusions\n  description: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.\n  manifestation: Even when I review the chart and there’s no evidence of a bad outcome, I keep believing that some routine delay or imperfect message from me has quietly harmed a patient and that everyone will eventually discover it.\n- key: S020\n  name: Hallucinations\n  description: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: When I’m alone after clinic or trying to fall asleep, I sometimes hear a patient’s voice as clearly as if they were in the room saying, 'You missed it,' and I’ll check the hallway or my phone even though nothing is there.\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I used to plan weekend hikes, sunrise photos, and big Indonesian dinners for friends, but for months now those things feel like obligations I can’t make myself care about.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: At work I move so slowly between rooms and sit staring at the EHR before clicking anything that nurses have started gently asking if I’m okay.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me, and I keep replaying small clinical imperfections as if they prove I’m a dangerous doctor.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I’ll read the same lab result or inbox message five times and still feel unsure what I just read, so even simple admin decisions can take me an embarrassing amount of time.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don’t have a plan to kill myself, but I catch myself thinking that it might be better if I didn’t wake up, especially after a day when I’m convinced I’ve let patients down.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the major depressive episode\n  manifestation: Before this depressive period after the clinic award, I had never heard patient voices or been convinced I had secretly harmed people despite evidence. The voices and fixed guilty beliefs only show up when I’m in this low, guilty state.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’m still showing up to clinic, but I’ve had to ask for help with non-urgent administrative work and I avoid social plans because I don’t trust my judgment or energy. It has started to affect how reliably I function at work and how connected I am outside of it.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and a month, starting after I received the clinic award.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / physician / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like these thoughts about your work or decisions keep coming back in a way that feels out of your control, even when you try not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do come back. I mean, not like a pop-up window or something, but… I’ll be doing something else and suddenly I’m back on some tiny clinical detail from earlier, some wording in a note or a decision that probably wasn’t even the main issue.\n\nAnd then it sort of turns into this bigger thing, like, “See, that’s proof you shouldn’t be trusted.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud. I know physicians are always talking about humility and double-checking, and in training they drill into you that confidence can be dangerous—one of my attendings used to say that constantly, actually, usually right before telling some horror story from residency.\n\nBut for me it’s not just learning from mistakes.     "content": "Candidate Diseases: \n[\"F42.2\", \"F43.10\"]\n\nInterview Transcript: \nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. How can I help you today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I keep feeling like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. Especially at work. I’m a doctor, so people just sort of hand you this enormous responsibility, and lately I keep replaying small things—like tiny clinical imperfections, things other people might say are normal—and I turn them over and over until they feel like proof that I’m dangerous, like I shouldn’t be allowed to do this.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds dramatic. It’s just… even when nothing major happened, I can’t shake the feeling that it means something terrible about me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these thoughts about not being good enough or being dangerous come up repeatedly, even when there’s no clear reason for them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, they do. It’s not always tied to something obvious. I’ll be driving home or trying to make dinner and suddenly I’m back on some small decision from work, some minor imperfection, and it starts feeling like evidence that I shouldn’t be trusted.\n\nAnd I know people say, you know, doctors are supposed to review things and be careful—one of my attendings used to say good clinicians worry, the bad ones don’t—but this feels different. It turns into this… certainty almost, that I’m dangerous somehow, or that I don’t deserve the responsibility people give me.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel intrusive and unwanted, like they’re not quite your own, and do you try to suppress or neutralize them in some way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. “Intrusive” is a word I hear patients use, and I know what it’s supposed to mean, but when it’s happening to me it doesn’t feel that clean.\n\nIt feels unwanted, yes, in the sense that I don’t want to be thinking about it. But it also feels like… like maybe I’m obligated to think about it, if that makes sense. Like if I stop turning it over, I’m being careless. I don’t know if that counts as suppressing or neutralizing.\n\nSometimes I try to distract myself, or I’ll go back through the situation in my head, but then I get pulled into the details—like one note I wrote last week, or the way someone phrased something in handover—and then I’m not really answering your question anymore. So… I guess I don’t know. I notice the thoughts, and I don’t like them, but I’m not sure what category they fit into.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you feel driven to do something—like checking, reviewing, or trying to fix the situation in your mind—to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say I’m “driven” exactly. I do go back over things in my head, but it’s hard to tell whether I’m trying to reduce anxiety or whether I’m just… thinking, like any careful doctor would.\n\nSometimes the line feels blurry. Like, in residency we were always told to double-check everything, and that becomes part of you. I had this senior once who could remember every lab value from rounds, and I used to think that was what competence looked like. So now when I replay something, I can tell myself it’s just diligence, but then it gets sort of sticky and I’m still there mentally long after it’s useful.\n\nBut whether I’m doing it to prevent something bad from happening… I’m not sure. I guess I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do you feel a strong need to do something—like re-reading notes, double-checking decisions, or going over the details again and again—to make sure everything is perfect or to ease your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it’s about making everything perfect. I mean, I do go back over details sometimes, but… medicine trains you to do that, doesn’t it? It’s hard to separate what’s reasonable from what’s me getting stuck.\n\nLike, I’ll think about a note or a decision and then suddenly I’m remembering something an attending said years ago about how missed details are where mistakes happen, and then I’m not really checking anything concrete anymore, I’m just… circling it. But whether that eases my mind? Not really. Or maybe briefly. I don’t know.\n\nI haven’t noticed a clear ritual, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I get pulled into reviewing things, and then I lose track of whether it’s useful.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly doing certain things—like re-reading notes, checking lab results multiple times, or going through decisions again and again in your head—not necessarily because you need to, but because it somehow feels necessary to avoid something bad happening or to calm the anxiety these thoughts bring up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can think of times I look back at things, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a pattern like… “if I don’t do this, something bad will happen.” It’s not that clear in my head.\n\nAnd with labs and notes, I mean, everyone checks things twice sometimes. There was a whole culture around that where I trained—people would praise you for being meticulous, and then later it becomes hard to tell when meticulous is just meticulous versus when it’s… something else.\n\nSo I guess I don’t really know. I don’t think I’m doing some specific repeated thing to calm anxiety, at least not that I can point to. I just get caught thinking about it sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself going over decisions or clinical details again and again, does it feel driven by a need to be certain that everything is right, or by a fear that something terrible might happen if you miss something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can separate those out cleanly. Sometimes it feels like wanting to be certain, but then I start thinking about what “certain” even means in clinical work, because there’s always ambiguity. I had an attending who used to say if you’re not uncomfortable, you’re not paying attention, which sounds wise until you’re lying awake thinking about some tiny detail from a chart.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “something terrible will happen if I don’t check this.” It’s more that I get caught in the reviewing, and the more I review, the less settled it feels. I can’t really tell if it’s fear or just habit or… trying to be careful.\n\nDoctor: When you go over clinical details or decisions repeatedly in your mind, does it feel like you’re trying to reach a sense of certainty or 'just right' feeling, even if you can’t clearly explain why it feels important?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Just right” is hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t say I’m chasing that exact feeling, like a switch flips and suddenly it’s okay. It’s more… I start turning something over, and then I remember how in training there was always this emphasis on not missing the one small thing, you know? Like the whole case could hinge on some lab value buried in the chart.\n\nBut I’m not sure that means I’m trying to get certainty. Sometimes I think I’m just trying to feel less uneasy, and sometimes I’m not even sure what I’m trying to do anymore. It doesn’t really land in a clear way for me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re going over these clinical details or decisions in your mind, do you feel anxious or distressed while you’re doing it, like something might go wrong if you don’t get it 'right'?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious in that exact way. Distressed, maybe, but it’s hard to tell if that’s because I think something will go wrong or because I’ve just been thinking about it too long.\n\nSometimes I’ll be going over something and then suddenly I’m remembering all these cases people talked about in training—like the cautionary tales, the “one missed detail” stories—and those kind of stick in your head. But I don’t know that I have a clear thought of, “If I don’t get this right, something bad will happen.” It’s more murky than that.\n\nI guess I do feel unsettled while I’m doing it, but I can’t really say what I’m afraid of, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself deliberately doing something—like re-reading notes, checking lab results, or going through decisions in your head in a specific way or for a certain amount of time—to try to reduce your distress or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do it in a structured way, if that’s what you mean. Like, I’m not counting how many times I read something or following a rule exactly.\n\nI might look back at something, or think through it again, but it’s hard to say whether I’m doing it to prevent something bad or just because my mind gets stuck on it. In the hospital, everyone checks things, you know—labs, notes, sign-outs—and sometimes it’s just part of the culture of not wanting to be sloppy. Though I guess that doesn’t really answer your question.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a specific ritual or set amount of time. It’s more vague than that.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts about clinical decisions or details ever interfered with your work, relationships, or daily activities, or caused you significant distress even when you try to let them go?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, though I keep wanting to qualify that because I’m still going in, I’m still showing up to clinic. It’s not like I’ve stopped working.\n\nBut the edges are fraying a bit. I’ve had to ask other people to help me with some of the non-urgent admin stuff, the things I normally would just handle. And then outside of work, I’ve been avoiding plans more. Someone will text about dinner or whatever, and I’ll just… I don’t trust that I’ll have the judgment or the energy to be decent company, I guess.\n\nSo yes, it’s started affecting how reliably I function at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people outside of it. Not in some dramatic way, but enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like these thoughts about your work or decisions keep coming back in a way that feels out of your control, even when you try not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do come back. I mean, not like a pop-up window or something, but… I’ll be doing something else and suddenly I’m back on some tiny clinical detail from earlier, some wording in a note or a decision that probably wasn’t even the main issue.\n\nAnd then it sort of turns into this bigger thing, like, “See, that’s proof you shouldn’t be trusted.” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud. I know physicians are always talking about humility and double-checking, and in training they drill into you that confidence can be dangerous—one of my attendings used to say that constantly, actually, usually right before telling some horror story from residency.\n\nBut for me it’s not just learning from mistakes. It feels like I keep replaying small imperfections as if they mean I’m a dangerous doctor. Like I don’t deserve the trust patients or colleagues put in me. I try to move on from it, but it loops back.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to mentally go over something in a specific way, like repeating certain thoughts or following a mental routine, to try to feel better or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that specific way. I mean, I do go back over things in my head, but it doesn’t feel like I have a set routine, like “repeat this phrase” or “review it three times” and then I’ll be safe.\n\nIt’s more messy than that. Like my brain just wanders back to it, and then I’m stuck there for a while. But I’m not deliberately doing a mental ritual, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI guess in medicine there’s so much internal rehearsal anyway—presenting patients, anticipating questions, thinking through differentials—and sometimes that blurs together. But no, I can’t point to a specific mental routine I feel compelled to follow.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts ever led you to avoid certain situations, responsibilities, or interactions because you were worried about not being able to manage them properly or being judged?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in the sense that I’m refusing to do my job — I’m still going to clinic, still seeing patients. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut I have been sort of sidestepping things. Like some of the non-urgent admin tasks, paperwork-type things I’d normally just knock out, I’ve asked other people for help with because I don’t feel like I can trust myself to handle everything cleanly right now. Which is embarrassing, because it’s not even the hard part of the job, you know?\n\nAnd socially, yes. I’ve been avoiding plans. Someone will suggest dinner or meeting up, and I think, I’m going to be strange, or tired, or I won’t have the judgment to respond like a normal person. Then I just don’t go, or I let the text sit there too long.\n\nSo… yes, I guess I am avoiding some responsibilities and interactions. I’m still functioning, but less reliably than I’d like, and I feel pretty disconnected outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more anxious or distressed if you try to stop yourself from going back over clinical details or decisions in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do. If I try to cut it off, it doesn’t really feel like relief, it feels like I’m being irresponsible somehow.\n\nLike if I don’t go back over the note, or the decision, or whatever tiny clinical thing is bothering me, then I’m ignoring evidence that I’m not safe to trust. Which—sorry, that sounds dramatic. I know everyone misses wording here and there, and I’ve had colleagues tell me, “That’s just being conscientious,” but it doesn’t feel like that.\n\nIt feels more like… I’m here because I can’t shake this sense that I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. So when I stop reviewing it, the distress goes up, because part of me thinks I’m letting myself off the hook too easily."
